Remove Duplicate Lines From a 48,000-Line CSV Without Losing the Original Order

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at meng4099244-ship-it.github.io

Last month I had to merge two exports from our billing system into one import file. The first export had 31,207 customer email addresses, the second had 17,706, and a few thousand lines overlapped. The CRM import endpoint rejects the whole file if it contains a single repeated email, so deduplication had to happen before upload.

There was a second constraint that tripped me up: row order drove import priority. Whichever line appeared first had to stay. So sort -u, the command half of my brain reached for, was off the table from the start. Here are three approaches that do work, tested on Ubuntu 22.04 with Python 3.11.1 and GNU awk 5.1.1.

The obvious one-liner is list(set(lines)). I've written it myself under deadline pressure. Don't. A set iterates in arbitrary hash order, so the output is a shuffled file, and the CRM import would run in whatever order the hash table happened to land on.

What you want is a seen set plus a counter:

Reading line by line means the file never sits in memory as one blob. The seen set held about 45,000 short strings, roughly 6 MB on my machine. I've pushed a 2 GB access log through the same shape of script by printing to stdout and redirecting; it took a few minutes but finished. Membership checks against a set are O(1) on average, which is why this stays fast as the file grows. The same pattern exists in JavaScript as a Set, so porting the script is a ten-minute job if your pipeline is Node-based.

When I just need the result and don't want to write a file, GNU awk does it in a single command:

$0 is the entire line. The expression seen[$0]++ returns 0 the first time a line shows up (falsy, so awk prints it) and counts upward after that. First occurrence wins, original order preserved. Same semantics as the Python script. The gawk manual has a whole chapter on how arrays and their uninitialized elements behave, worth a read if this idiom feels too magical.

One honest caveat: awk accumulates every line it has seen in memory, same as the Python set. For a 40 GB file on a 4 GB box neither will end well, and you'd reach for sort with a stable flag or a disk-based approach. For anything under a few hundred megabytes it's fine.

Sometimes the input arrives through Slack as a pasted list of a few thousand lines, from a teammate on Windows who has no intention of opening a terminal. For those, I use a free online duplicate line remover. Paste, click, copy the result back out.

This browser-based tool deletes duplicate lines while keeping the first occurrence and the original order, which is the same semantics as the two methods above. The processing happens client-side in the page, so the paste never leaves the machine. With customer email addresses involved, that's the part I actually care about, and it's faster than explaining to a colleague how to run awk in PowerShell.

The second export came out of a Windows service. Every line ended in \r\n, and my rstrip("\n") left the carriage return in place. On screen the lines looked identical; in memory they weren't:

First run reported dropped 3203. After I changed the strip to line.rstrip("\r\n"), the same file reported dropped 3847. Those extra 644 "duplicates" were the same addresses, invisible until the repr showed the \r.

The export was saved as "UTF-8 with BOM", which many spreadsheet tools do by default. The very first line carries a three-byte prefix that repr makes easy to spot:

'\ufeffalice@example.com' never equals 'alice@example.com', so if that address appears again later, it survives as a duplicate. Opening the file with encoding="utf-8-sig" strips the BOM on read and the problem disappears. I now check the first byte of any vendor CSV before writing the script.

A teammate "fixed" one of these files with sort -u in a cron job. Dedup worked. The import priority did not, because the output came back sorted, not in original order. Locale changes the collateral damage, too:

Same input, same machine, two different outputs depending on LC_ALL. The GNU coreutils sort documentation covers the field and locale behavior in detail. When order matters, dedupe with awk or the streaming script instead, and save sort -u for cases where sorted output is what you wanted anyway.

For files in the gigabytes I use the Python script. For quick shell work, awk. For a pasted list from a colleague, the browser tool. All three preserve the first occurrence, and that's usually the only thing the downstream system actually checks.

More free browser-based utilities at utilvy.com.

Reading a 2 km laser rangefinder module over UART with Python (LR2000E2, 55 AA protocol)

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A laser rangefinder module is one of the easiest long-range sensors to wire up and one of the easiest to misread. The wiring is four wires. The mistakes happen in the protocol: a frame header you skip, a checksum you do not verify, a unit you assume. This post walks through a small, complete Python reader for the ERDI LR2000E2, a 905 nm UART laser rangefinder module with a published range of 4 to 2,000 m, and shows where the traps are.

Everything below comes from the LR2000E2 user manual (v1.2, public edition 2026-09-06) and the product page at https://erdilrf.com/models/lr2000e2/. Data checked on 2026-11-06.

Two numbers matter for software. The rate is adaptive, so you should not build a 10 Hz control loop around it; treat it as a slow, long-reach sensor. And the "valid measurement rate" in the manual is 98 % or better up to 400 m and 80 % or better from 400 to 2,000 m, which means at long range one reading in five can legitimately be a failure. Your code has to handle failed readings as a normal case, not an exception.

Two practical notes. First, the manual states the 3.3–5 V supply range but does not separately specify the logic level of TXD and RXD. If you power the module at 5 V and talk to a 3.3 V-only microcontroller pin, measure TXD with a scope or meter first, or ask the manufacturer for the model-specific statement. A USB-serial adapter that supports 3.3 V levels is the safe starting point. Second, pin 6 is an enable input: the module expects it high to operate, so tie it according to the manual before wondering why nothing answers.

The inrush current is below 400 mA, so use a supply that can deliver it without sagging. A weak USB hub that browns out at power-on will look like a protocol problem.

Frames are 8 bytes. A command is 55 AA D1 D2 D3 D4 , where the checksum is the low byte of the sum of the function code and the four data bytes. The reply is also 8 bytes: 55 AA STA FF DIS_H DIS_L . STA = 1 means success; any other value means the measurement failed. The distance is DIS_H * 256 + DIS_L, and the manual states that all results are output as the actual value × 10, so you divide by 10 to get metres.

You can check the checksum column by hand: 0x88 + 4 × 0xFF = 0x484, low byte 0x84.

sync() instead of ser.read(8). Reading a fixed 8 bytes works until the first dropped byte, after which every frame is misaligned and you decode garbage forever. Searching for 55 AA first costs nothing and removes that failure class. Note the small gap: a data byte pair of 55 AA inside a payload would false-trigger, which is why the strict checksum option exists.

strict defaults to off. The manual text describes the reply checksum as the sum from the first header byte to DATA4. Enable strict=True on your bench first and log what you see; if your unit's replies agree with the formula, leave it on in production. If they do not, you have found a document or firmware difference worth reporting to the supplier before you design anything around it.

Failed readings are normal. ok=False is returned for a bad frame and for STA != 1. Keep the last good value with a timestamp and a staleness limit instead of passing None downstream.

Units. Dividing by 10 is specific to this model family. Other modules from other vendors, and even other ERDI families, report millimetres or centimetres. Never copy a divisor from a sibling model; take it from that model's manual.

You do not need a 2 km line of sight to test the software. Point the module at a wall at a known distance such as 5 m or 20 m, measure with a tape, and log 100 single shots. Plot the histogram. You are checking three things: the mean against the tape, the spread against the ±1 m accuracy band, and the fraction of STA != 1 readings. For targets that are well inside the range, the failure fraction should be very small. If it is not, check supply sag and the SW-SHOT line before suspecting the optics.

For the long-range claim, treat the datasheet range as a statement about a target and conditions, not a promise. The accuracy table does not specify target reflectivity and size, and real reach depends on weather, target colour, angle and sun. A low-reflectivity target at an oblique angle will fall short of 2,000 m. Plan for margin.

If you would rather not maintain this yourself, ERDI publishes an open-source driver set for its 905 nm modules (Python, Arduino and ROS 2) at https://github.com/ErdiTech/erdi-lrf-drivers. Each constant in it is cited to the model's manual, which is the point: the unit divisor, baud rate and frame length are per model, not per family.

What is the maximum range of the LR2000E2? The manufacturer's table gives 4 to 2,000 m as the standard range, with accuracy of ±1 m up to 400 m and 0.4 % of distance beyond. Actual reach depends on target reflectivity, size and weather.

What baud rate does it use? 115200 bps, 8 data bits, frame length 8 bytes.

How do I convert the reading to metres? Combine the two data bytes into an integer and divide by 10.

Can I power it from 5 V? The supply range is 3.3 to 5 V. The signal-pin logic level is not stated separately in the manual, so verify before connecting to a 3.3 V-only input.

Is it eye-safe? The manufacturer states Class 1. Check the model's own document and local regulations for your product.

What does it cost? USD 120 per unit for 1 to 30 pieces, with shipping included, as published on the product page on 2026-11-06.

Salesforce vs HubSpot vs Pipedrive for Small Teams: How the Three Actually Differ

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 6 • Originally published at saasstatshub.com

The three entry plans are different shapes of product, and that — not the per-seat price — is usually the deciding factor. Pipedrive Lite ($14/seat/month annual) is a focused sales-pipeline tool. HubSpot Starter ($7/seat/month annual offer price for new customers, $20 normal) is a five-hub starter bundle that adds starter-level marketing, service, and content tools to the CRM. Salesforce Starter Suite ($25/user/month) is a simplified all-in-one CRM whose main draw is the upgrade path into the Salesforce ecosystem.

One feature splits them sharply: email sync with your inbox is included at HubSpot Starter, starts at Pipedrive Growth ($39), and is not confirmed for Salesforce Starter Suite on its official pages.

Read this as "what the official pages state," not as an exhaustive feature audit. The email-sync row is the one most likely to eliminate a vendor outright — if your team expects email conversations inside the CRM, Pipedrive's entry price for that need is the Growth plan, not Lite.

Qualifiers that travel with these numbers: HubSpot's $7/$10 are offer prices with renewal terms not stated; Salesforce labels its prices "starting price" and marks its page "subject to change"; Pipedrive's monthly rate was not verifiable. Put your must-have feature list next to the numbers before comparing.

The full comparison, with sources and the pricing checklist, is on SaaSStatsHub: https://saasstatshub.com/crm/salesforce-vs-hubspot-vs-pipedrive/

Originally published with full sources at SaaSStatsHub. Free calculators for MRR, churn, CAC and LTV: https://saasstatshub.com/tools/

Wabi Not for You? Pick the Right Alternative by What You Actually Need

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

I'm the author of Wabi Wiki, an unofficial guide to the Wabi AI assistant — not affiliated with Wabi or Replika. This post is a rewritten version of our alternatives guide, written for Dev.to.

Wabi is a multiplayer personal AI assistant that builds mini-apps inside your chats — genuinely different from anything else out there. But different isn't the same as right for you. It launched publicly on October 19, 2026, it's free and English-first, and it runs on iPhone, Android and Mac only. If any of that rules it out for you, here's the honest map of where to look instead, sorted by the need Wabi doesn't meet.

As of October 2026 there is no Windows app and no web version of Wabi, and none has been announced. If your day happens on a Windows PC, the practical answer today is a mainstream assistant in the browser — ChatGPT, Claude or Gemini. You lose the multiplayer threads and the auto-built mini-apps, which are the whole reason Wabi is interesting. You gain an assistant that actually runs on the machine in front of you. If those group mini-apps are the point, run Wabi on your phone and treat the PC as a spectator for now.

Wabi is built to get things done, not to be your friend. If what you actually want is one-on-one conversation and emotional companionship, look at AI companion apps — Replika first, since it comes from Wabi's own founder. Expect freemium: the chat is free, the deeper relationship features sit behind a subscription. Our Wabi Wiki comparison of the two lays out which job each one is actually built for.

Wabi is days old and its maker openly says the business model isn't decided yet. Fun for a side experiment; risky as the backbone of a critical workflow. If AI is load-bearing in your job today, pick an established assistant or productivity tool with a paid plan and a track record as your primary — and let Wabi earn its way in over time.

Sometimes the honest answer is that you don't need an AI at all. A shared calendar, a shared list and a group chat handle coordination without connecting anyone's bank account to anything. Wabi's edge is doing the legwork for the group. If you don't need the legwork done, the simple tools win on trust and cost.

Still leaning Wabi? Read the full alternatives guide on Wabi Wiki, then follow the safe download steps — and if privacy is what's holding you back, read the safety facts first.

Come creare un bot Telegram che risponde solo a te, con il long polling in Python

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Un bot Telegram è un comodo telecomando per uno script o un agente IA sulla tua macchina. Funziona su qualsiasi telefono, non richiede una tua app e non richiede un server pubblico. Il problema è che un bot è pubblico. Chiunque può trovarne lo username e scrivergli, e il token dimostra soltanto che il tuo script è il bot; non dice nulla su chi sta scrivendo. L'app desktop osFoundry ha un ponte del genere per i suoi agenti, scritto in Rust (telegram_bridge.rs). Qui sotto trovi lo stesso schema in circa 70 righe di Python con la sola libreria standard, testato contro un sostituto locale della Bot API, insieme all'errore di una riga che ha trasformato un bot tranquillo in 183.389 richieste al minuto.

Telegram consegna i messaggi in due modi. Un webhook richiede un URL HTTPS pubblico che Telegram possa chiamare. getUpdates non richiede di esporre nulla: lo script chiede i nuovi messaggi e, con timeout=25, Telegram tiene aperta la richiesta fino a 25 secondi e risponde appena ne arriva uno. Su un portatile è la scelta giusta. Nessuna porta da aprire, nessun tunnel, nessun certificato.

Il timeout HTTP (40 s) deve essere più lungo dell'attesa del long poll (25 s). Se è più corto, ogni ciclo tranquillo finisce con un timeout lato client che sembra un'interruzione. L'app conserva il token nel portachiavi del sistema operativo, non in un file. Da Python, keyring.get_password fa lo stesso, e per un test va bene una variabile d'ambiente.

In 60 secondi di inattività, il ciclo di long poll ha fatto 3 richieste e uno short poll ogni secondo ne ha fatte 60. Un messaggio arrivato mentre una richiesta era tenuta aperta ha ricevuto la risposta da 1,5 a 6,3 ms dopo nel test locale. Su una rete reale si aggiunge il tragitto di andata e ritorno verso Telegram.

Il bot deve sapere quale persona serve prima di fare qualsiasi cosa. L'app lo fa con un codice monouso. Clicchi "Pair a phone" sulla macchina, che mostra otto caratteri casuali, e dal telefono invii /pair CODE al bot. Chi invia un codice valido entro 10 minuti è il proprietario.

Il codice viene da secrets, non da qualcosa di indovinabile come l'ora o il nome del bot, perché il codice è l'unica cosa che autorizza un mittente. Viene anche rimosso quando è usato, quindi funziona una sola volta.

Quello che viene salvato è il from.id numerico, non l'@username né il nome visualizzato. Entrambi possono essere cambiati, e uno username liberato può essere registrato da qualcun altro. Accanto viene salvato l'id della chat, per un motivo che compare più sotto.

Tre regole reggono la sicurezza, e ciascuna nasce da un errore specifico annotato nel sorgente dell'app.

I mittenti sconosciuti ricevono silenzio. Una risposta come "you are not paired" dice a un estraneo che il bot è collegato alla macchina di una persona reale e lo invita a continuare a provare. Nessuna risposta sembra un bot che nessuno esegue.

Il pairing funziona solo in una chat privata. Un bot aggiunto a un gruppo riceve i messaggi del gruppo, quindi fare il pairing lì permetterebbe a ogni membro di dare istruzioni all'agente.

A una persona associata si risponde solo nella chat da cui ha fatto il pairing. I commenti dell'app descrivono una versione precedente che controllava il mittente ma rispondeva alla chat da cui arrivava il messaggio. Un utente associato che aggiungeva il bot a un gruppo si ritrovava con un agente che rispondeva in quel gruppo, dove tutti potevano usare strumenti sulla macchina del proprietario. L'autorizzazione è per mittente e la risposta va a una chat, quindi i due controlli vanno fatti insieme.

/unpair viene gestito prima di quel controllo sulla chat, così l'accesso si può togliere dal telefono, che è dove sei quando ti serve.

offset è il modo in cui lo script dice a Telegram quali aggiornamenti ha visto. Tutto ciò che sta sotto è confermato e scartato, e tutto ciò che è pari o sopra viene inviato di nuovo. Il commento su quella riga è la parte importante. È allettante spostare l'offset solo dopo aver fatto qualcosa con il messaggio, perché sembra "elaborato". Ma il messaggio di un estraneo viene ignorato di proposito, quindi non viene mai confermato, quindi ritorna alla chiamata successiva, subito, perché c'è un aggiornamento in sospeso e Telegram non ha motivo di tenere la richiesta aperta.

Il backoff serve per l'altro guasto: un token revocato o digitato male fallisce a ogni richiesta, e riprovare a piena velocità significa un 401 ogni pochi secondi, per sempre. La scala di 5, 15, 60 e poi 300 secondi è quella che usa l'app.

Il sostituto locale implementa getUpdates (conferma tramite offset e attesa del timeout) e sendMessage, e registra ogni chiamata. Ogni scenario ha iniettato un messaggio e poi letto ciò che il bot rispondeva.

Poi quattro cicli sono stati eseguiti per 60 secondi, ciascuno contro il proprio sostituto:

Sul sostituto, che risponde dalla stessa macchina, il bug girava a circa 3.000 richieste al secondo. Contro la vera API, il ciclo andrebbe veloce quanto permette il tragitto di rete, con lo stesso "hi" dell'estraneo riconsegnato a ogni chiamata e nulla di più recente in arrivo. Basta un messaggio di qualcuno a cui non risponderai mai per far partire tutto.

Un vero ponte deve anche limitare le risposte sotto il limite di 4.096 caratteri di Telegram (l'app taglia a 3.900, su un confine di carattere, perché un taglio a byte può cadere nel mezzo di un carattere giapponese) e inviare le risposte come testo semplice. Con il Markdown, Telegram rifiuta l'intero messaggio se un asterisco o un trattino basso non è bilanciato, e un agente che scrive di codice in snake_case produce esattamente questo. Soprattutto, il testo in arrivo è scritto da chi ha il telefono in mano, quindi deve arrivare all'agente come input non attendibile, come un'email. Il pairing decide chi può parlare con l'agente; non rende sicuro ciò che viene detto.

L'app desktop osFoundry include questo ponte per i suoi agenti IA: Device → Background per il token e "Pair a phone" nel pannello Agents di un canale. È gratuita dalla pagina di download di osFoundry.

EXCLUSIVE: Ryan Serhant Warns Homebuyers Against Waiting for 2027: ‘You Don’t Want to Wait Until It’s Too Late’

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ryan Serhant is warning would-be homebuyers against assuming that lower mortgage rates will deliver an easier path to homeownership in 2027—and says the housing market has a problem that cheaper borrowing alone cannot solve.

Speaking exclusively to Realtor.com®, Serhant shared his outlook for the year ahead, identified markets he believes could surprise buyers, and weighed in on the debate over private listings.

He also offered a glimpse inside his own townhouse and revealed a buyer request that has him searching for an enormous stretch of land with its own airport.

At the center of his housing outlook is a concern about people at opposite ends of the property ladder being unable to move.

“We have entered an environment where people can't afford to buy their first home, and their parents can't afford to leave their last one, and it's a problem we need to solve," he said.

“Lower mortgage rates will help, but rates alone are not going to fix the cost of housing or the cost of moving. We need to create more supply and fewer obstacles," Serhant added.

Serhant argued that the system has become an obstacle to moving, with consequences that have developed over many years.

“I think that what we've created is a environment, that is punishing mobility. Instead of rewarding mobility. And this was set up a long, long time ago. We didn't get here overnight.”

“Like, the housing market and the system we operate in rewards stability, because what's… it makes everyone feel comfortable. But then when that stability does not move, because stability is stability, mobility is mobility. Then everyone gets angry, and that's the environment that we're currently in.”

For his outlook, he is watching whether households have both the confidence and the resources to act.

“I think I'm watching employment, and I'm watching household confidence right now. I think pent-up demand only becomes a transaction when people have the confidence and the financial ability to act, and we'll see if that happens anytime soon.”

For people who can afford to buy but are considering waiting for a better deal in 2027, Serhant pointed to opportunities he believes already exist.

“I think in a lot of markets, we've entered a buyer's market now. You have the ability to negotiate in markets that you haven't been able to negotiate in in the past couple years.”

“I think there's a great opportunity to use the rate pressure that we have to negotiate closing costs, to negotiate rate buy-downs, to negotiate lower purchase prices, to get a house that really, really, really works for you. Remember, it is such an old and annoying saying, but you do marry the house, and you do date through the rates.”

His argument rests on the potential trade-off between borrowing costs and competition, although his comments about future rates and inventory are predictions.

“The market does become very stagnant. Interest rates should come down, and remember, you can refinance. And you should use the opportunity right now, if you can afford it, to purchase in a high-interest rate environment, because there's less competition.”

“Everyone wants low rates, they never want what comes with it. You know what'll happen if rates come down? There will be no houses for sale.”

“You won't even have the opportunity to complain about a high monthly payment. You won't even be able to talk about—the lack of affordability. You won't be able to complain, because there will be nothing for sale, because the demand is so great. There is not nearly enough houses for the demand we have in this country. Every 40-year-old who still lives at home will then move.”

Serhant also argued that the circumstances accompanying lower rates can make buyers reluctant to purchase.

“And what also brings down low interest rates is job insecurity. You know, is unemployment, is war. Are things that happen in the global environment that will make people, not confident to go and make a massive purchase. And so, you might miss that opportunity, too.”

He used the early months of the pandemic to illustrate his point about confidence and timing.

“In the spring of 2020, that was a great time to buy. But no, we had COVID, 'You can't buy. There's a death ticker on the bottom of every TV screen. You might not live.' You didn't take advantage of this environment, and then when the market came back, it was too late.”

“And so, I would use this moment to move quickly, because when things do even out, and they always do, markets go up and markets go down. Always. And forever. You don't want to wait until it's too late.”

Serhant highlighted returning city residents and investment in jobs as themes behind his market picks.

“I think people are returning to cities in a way that we haven't seen before. It's one of the reasons we just were launching, officially Chicago. This coming Wednesday. I'm incredibly excited about that.”

“The demand and the volume we've seen from all of our other Serhant markets wanting to move back to Chicago, who moved out over the past couple years. Taking advantage of good value, like we just talked about, is really exciting.”

“And I think places where you're seeing incredible job growth. Areas like Ohio, where there's major, major tech investment, not just in data centers. In office. And in manufacturing.”

“We have a lot of investment that's going into Western Pennsylvania, and, you're also seeing a lot of movement into Alabama.”

“You know, I think there are markets that will really, really surprise you because of great quality of life for the best cost structure the country has to offer.”

Serhant also addressed private listings, beginning with his explanation of how they differ from properties marketed publicly.

“A private listing is the opposite of a public to the consumer listing," he said.

“It has been the standard over the past… 20-some-odd years—that if you want to market your home, so that there is a fair and free market and competitive marketplace, that if you opt to work with a real estate agent. That you have to bring your home to the public marketplace.”

“Obviously, every home that's not on the market is technically a private listing. The Empire State Building is a private listing, for what it's worth. The whole world is a private listing.”

He then offered his criticism of listing restrictions and penalties. The following is his characterization of the debate, rather than a description of a single policy applying to every platform or multiple listing service.

“But what has changed now is the MLSs, and the listing aggregators have installed fees and penalties.”

“So, if you list with them, as you're supposed to, you can have the prize of being public, but if you don't list with them, because you want to work with an agent, but you don't want your home to be blasted all over the world, then you can never list with them ever, and we will also punish you, potentially with fines.”

“The current debate is over, should that be allowed? Who actually owns the data, and who actually owns the property? And access to it. Is it the brokerage? Is it the websites that showcase listings? Is it the multiple listing services? Is it the owner of the property?”

For Serhant, the discussion should focus on what sellers receive from the process.

“And that's what the debate is over right now, is what they say, consumer choice. I think what is broken is that it's not about consumer choice. I think everyone's focused on the wrong thing. I think people should be focused on consumer value.”

“Where's the upside? Where's the consumer upside? You know, the battle right now is over commission fees, and who gets a piece. And I think that's gross, and I'm in this business.”

“My position is pretty simple. I think your home deserves the whole market if you'd like it to. And if a seller genuinely wants privacy, we need to respect that. But then you have to explain the trade-off. Privacy and maximum exposure are different objectives.”

On concerns that private listings could reduce a seller's pool of buyers or disadvantage people without the right connections, Serhant acknowledged the risks.

“I think there are real concerns. But I think everybody has to be fully aware of what those concerns are. And again, I think it should be up to the consumer, and unfortunately, these rules exist because in a world where there is no rules, and there is no punishment. Not everyone is a good actor.”

He drew on his own experience working as an agent during the financial crisis.

“Like, all the people that are leading this fight, none of them were real estate agents like I was in 2008 and 2009. None of them were trying to get business on Craigslist. None of them were trying to figure out what was for sale and what wasn't for sale.”

“None of them were trying to figure out if the listing agent in front of them was lying to their client or not. None of them were trying to figure out, with the fiduciary responsibility of a buyer in front of you, how to give them the most accurate and up-to-date information.”

“None of them were, like me, on the streets of New York City, or trying to do deals in Florida and California, trying to figure out what is actually for sale, and if a buyer isn't paying an excise commission by working with that agent, they actually won't even get that property? Is that really? What we want to go back to?”

“I feel like a homeowner's value and a homeowner's biggest asset shouldn't become a real estate brokerage's, or an MLS's, or a listing aggregator's recruiting tool.”

Away from the business of buying and selling, Serhant shared the first thing he does when he walks into his townhouse.

“I yell out my daughter's name. I wanted to see where she is in my house. We have a townhouse, so I'm trying to figure out which floor she's on, so I can, you know, prepare.”

His favorite spaces include a gym on the lower level, although his affection extends to the whole property.

“I have a pretty awesome gym. In the lower level that I built out, that's really, really cool, and I think that's pretty awesome, but I mean, I built it with my bare hand. It was a TV show that we did for Bravo about our renovation, and I love that house. Whole thing is great.”

He also described the practical details that help make his kitchen work, starting with a concealed ladder for reaching high cabinets.

“We have incredibly high ceilings. So we have a hidden ladder in our kitchen that looks like the baseboard under the kitchen. Island, and it pops out with the touch of your toe.”

“And, the ladder shows up, and so for anyone, you know, you know, who's vertically challenged to be able to reach upper cabinetry without having a ladder in front of your face at all times, so that you can still enjoy all that light and sunshine and high ceilings, I think is key.”

“Probably the greatest things that we have also in that kitchen are touchless, so, like, the sink, you know, you're cooking, and your hands are messy, and you're always hitting the thing. We have the kitchen sink, where all you have to do is tap it, and it turns on.”

“And so you just hit it with the back of your hand, and it turns on. So you're not, like, also getting the sink and the faucet dirty.”

“And then the same thing for the kitchen refrigerator and freezer. All you do is you slowly kind of, like, tap it with your elbow, or with the side, so you're not, like, yanking on anything, and it automatically just reveals itself to you. I think those little touches are overlooked.”

Among the unusual requests Serhant receives, one current search involves a vast amount of land—and a private airport.

“They're all wild. I'm dealing with somebody right now that wants 100,000 acres of land, which is impossible, for the most part."

For all the features a buyer might want, Serhant has one clear priority when choosing a home.

“You can change everything about the house you want over the years, you can grow into it, you can grow out of it, you can update it, you can paint it, you can flip it upside down. Unless you are Ken Griffin, and you can pick up a house, and you can move it, for the most part. Change the location.”

“And so, you always buy location first. Whatever that is, wherever that place is you want to be for the next 5-10 years, and most times you're paying more. But no one ever paid too much. And regretted it years later. Very, very rarely does that happen.”

“Remember, if you buy a trophy, you get to sell a trophy. If you buy a discount, because you're cheap, you will have to sell a discount. And you will feel cheap.”

Kelsi Karruli is a senior celebrity reporter who joined Realtor.com in 2025. She writes about trends encompassing real estate and celebrity properties. She previously wrote for Daily Mail, True Urban Culture, and JMedia Corporation, where she specialized in covering celebrities, relationships, psychology, and gender issues. She has a bachelor's degree in journalism from Pace University, where she received the James Rose Award for excellence in journalism. When she isn’t updating readers on A-listers' moves, she is spending time with her cat, Mary, and cheering on the Albanian soccer team.

CBS Officially Gives Mike Colter's New Legal Thriller a Sunday Night Takeover

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mike Colter might be best known as Marvel's Luke Cage, but for much of his career, he's called CBS home. Under creators Robert and Michelle King, he's had three prominent roles on the Eye network, starting with their iconic Emmy-winning political legal drama The Good Wife, where he played the notorious Chicago drug kingpin, Lemond Bishop. The Kings then made him part of the main ensemble in their ambitious supernatural mystery series Evil, opposite Katja Herbers for four seasons of unraveling mysteries from the inexplicable to the downright demonic. Their latest legal thriller now has him back in the spotlight, and CBS is about to give him and Rachel Keller their biggest spotlight yet.

Cupertino has been off to a solid start on Thursday nights alongside the Kings' other series, Elsbeth. Each week sees Michael Price (Colter) and Olivia Siffre (Keller), two lawyers cheated by Silicon Valley and fired from their jobs at a tech start-up, taking on cases with their associates at their up-and-coming firm against the goliaths of the tech industry on behalf of the people they've long harmed with impunity. As of its second week, the series ranked as the second-most-watched new series of the season so far, racking up 6.8 million viewers from October 12-18, according to Nielsen's live-plus-seven-day measure. This Sunday, Price, Siffre & Associates will aim to get even more viewers into the legal action by taking over CBS for the evening.

In addition to Thursday night's episode "Celeste," airing at 10 p.m. ET/PT, three back-to-back episodes of Cupertino will play after 60 Minutes in place of the usually scheduled Marshals and Tracker. That includes one new episode, titled "Harper," airing in the Yellowstone spin-off's spot at 8 p.m. ET/PT, followed by re-airings of the first two episodes, "Michael & Olivia" and "Vik & Bo." Another new episode, "Yungwood," will then follow next Thursday, November 12. The pre-emption will use the break from the usual Sunday action slate to give other viewers a larger sample of a sci-fi legal thriller premise that critics have powered to a stellar 100%.

"Harper" puts a high-stakes twist on the courtroom thrills when a traffic jam forces everyone involved in Michael and Olivia's latest case to have to fight in the legal battle remotely from their cars. The case in question hits close to home for Christy (Ella Stiller), as her roommate finds herself denied a critical transplant after an AI algorithm swaps her place on the list with a wealthy man. The only hope remaining is for the lawyers to find a judge who will swiftly stop the operation from going forward, but that's easier said than done. "Yungwood," meanwhile, brings in a big guest star in Method Man to play the titular legendary rapper whose revenue is jeopardized by an AI-music studio generating copycats of his music. However, Michael and Olivia's fight for art and music gets personal when Michael's own father, Bayard (Joe Morton), takes the stand as the opposing counsel. Busy Philipps, Nik Dodani, Renée Elise Goldsberry, and Rob McClure also star.

Cupertino airs tonight on CBS, with a new episode this Sunday, November 8, at 8 p.m. ET/PT, followed by two more reruns. Stay tuned here at Collider for more on the future of the Kings' latest hit.

A New Era of Bionic Vision? AI Helps Scientists Unlock the Brain’s Visual Cortex

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Researchers have used artificial intelligence to improve how a brain implant stimulates the visual cortex of a blind person, potentially advancing the development of bionic eyes.

Artificial intelligence could make future bionic eyes more precise and responsive by improving how electrical signals communicate with the brain. In a study published in Neuron, researchers used deep learning to control brain stimulation in a blind man, producing more accurate neural responses with less electrical current.

The findings could advance visual prostheses that bypass damaged eyes and optic nerves, stimulating the visual cortex directly to generate perceptions of light.

Researchers from UC Santa Barbara, ETH Zurich, and Miguel Hernández University (MHU) tested the approach in a 27-year-old man who lost his sight following a traumatic brain injury. At a hospital in Spain, a temporary implant containing 96 electrodes stimulated his visual cortex, producing phosphenes, perceived as flashes or shapes of light.

Led in part by UCSB professor Michael Beyeler, the team trained a deep neural network to predict brain responses to different stimulation patterns. The AI model also incorporated measurements of resting brain activity, allowing it to account for changes in the brain’s condition before each test.

The AI-designed stimulation patterns produced targeted neural responses more accurately and required less electrical current than comparison approaches. Researchers also found that recorded brain activity predicted the participant’s visual perceptions better than the electrical settings alone.

“Engineers naturally want to treat phosphenes like pixels: stimulate more electrodes, and you should get a more complete image,” Beyeler said. “But the brain does not work that way. Electrodes interact, neural responses fluctuate, and what we put into the brain is not necessarily what the person perceives. The challenge is to learn more about that transformation.”

The implant could both stimulate neurons and record their responses, enabling the AI to learn from actual brain activity. The participant also described perceived flashes, including their brightness, size, shape, and color.

The technology could eventually help people who lose their sight through injuries or neurological and inherited conditions. Beyeler emphasized those “who have been able to see for part of their life, but then, because of an inherited eye disease or an accident, have lost their vision,” adding, “For those people, the desire may be very strong to get some vision back.”

Describing the international research effort as “a true collaboration,” Beyeler said the study demonstrated how computational models could influence experiments involving human participants.

“Building a model in the abstract is one thing,” he said. “Seeing it shape an experiment with a person is something else entirely. That ability to go from theory to something that may one day help people is what drives much of the work in our lab.”

Although the research remains an early proof of concept, it suggests that future visual prostheses may work better by continuously adjusting stimulation to an individual’s neural responses.

“A useful visual prosthesis cannot rely on a fixed recipe,” Beyeler said. “It has to learn how an individual brain responds and adapt the stimulation accordingly. Ultimately, the device should adapt to the person, not the other way around.”

Reference: “Deep learning-based control of electrically evoked activity in human visual cortex” by Pehuén Moure, Jacob Granley, Fabrizio Grani, Leili Soo, Antonio Lozano, Rocío López-Peco, Adrián Villamarín-Ortiz, Cristina Soto-Sánchez, Shih-Chii Liu, Michael Beyeler and Eduardo Fernández, 7 August 2026, Neuron. DOI: 10.1016/j.neuron.2026.07.006

Apple explains how to turn off the new MacBook Pro’s glowing logo

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

While the illuminated Apple logo on the new MacBook Pro drew one of the biggest cheers during last week’s announcement, Apple knows that not everyone will want to keep it on. Here’s the company’s official guide to turning it off.

The new MacBook Pro with PROLED XDR Display marks the return of the illuminated Apple logo on its lid after a decade.

As Chance Miller explained in his review of the new MacBook Pro, “unlike older MacBooks, which used light from the display’s backlight to illuminate the logo, the new OLED MacBook Pro has no backlight to draw from, requiring a different approach.”

He added that, as with the old Macs, the logo’s brightness is tied to the screen brightness, though it also accounts for overall ambient brightness. He also noted that “when the MacBook Pro is closed, the logo looks like polished metal, just like it has since the 2016 redesign.”

The review echoed some of the findings developer Gui Rambo shared on X a few days ago:

The glowing Apple logo on the new MacBook Pro is controllable by software. It has 25kHz PWM and per-device calibration. CoreBrightness controls the relationship between screen and logo brightness. The brightness of the logo is adjusted differently depending on the enclosure color pic.twitter.com/baqBEkzJUo

Now, Apple has published an official support document that also explains how the illuminated logo works and how to turn it off completely.

The Apple logo behind the built-in display is illuminated whenever the Mac is awake. It automatically dims or brightens based on the brightness level of the built-in screen and the lighting conditions detected by the computer’s ambient light sensor. For all screen-brightness levels, the Apple logo will be dimmer in a dim room and brighter in a bright room.

For users who’d rather keep the logo turned off at all times, Apple explains that they can run the following command in Terminal, provided they have the administrator password:

sudo defaults write com.apple.CoreBrightness LEBacklightMode -int 0

To revert the command and have the logo be illuminated again, users will need to run the following command:

sudo defaults delete com.apple.CoreBrightness LEBacklightMode

Apple notes that reinstalling macOS will also turn the logo illumination back on.

To read Apple’s full support article, follow this link. And make sure to also catch 9to5Mac’s review of the new MacBook Pro.

CoreCivic (CXW) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Facility sales funded debt cuts and a $500 million buyback, reshaping the balance sheet.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

CoreCivic, Inc. (CXW -7.50%) reported third quarter 2026 financial results highlighted by the execution of strategic real estate divestitures and a significant reduction in corporate debt. Management reported that the company completed the sale of four detention facilities to the federal government for gross proceeds of $2.2 billion, utilizing the net proceeds to retire over $700 million in indebtedness and launch a $500 million accelerated share repurchase program. The company reported that adjusted diluted earnings per share increased 104% compared to the prior year quarter, supported by an occupancy rate of 84% in the residential segment and the contribution of Clinical Solutions Pharmacy. Management indicated that ICE populations nationwide have approached record levels in the second half of 2026, while the company continues to maintain 5,500 beds of idle capacity for future government needs.

We are focused on maintaining the company's forward momentum by delivering on this mission.

On this morning's call, we will discuss our third quarter operational results and provide updates on the latest developments with our government partners. Who will provide greater detail on our third quarter 26 financial results as well as our updated 2026 financial guidance. Before we discuss this quarter's financial performance, I want to reemphasize the value creation events that occurred during the quarter.

In the third quarter of 26, we announced the sale of 4 facilities to the Department of Homeland Security for after tax net proceeds of approximately $1.6 billion These sales were undertaken at an average price of $307 thousand per bed ranging from $371 thousand per bed at the Otay Mesa facility in California to $231 thousand per bed at the Midwest facility in Kansas. The federal government determined the fair market value of these properties by engaging independent appraisers who follow the government mandated yellow book standards as required by federal statute. These rigorous standards direct appraisers to base value on replacement cost under provisions specifically designed to determine a fair market value for special purpose highly improved properties.

This methodology incorporates a variety of relevant factors, including design, condition, age, location, size, available land, and expansion capacity. Under this federal statutory framework, the sales price rests on the appraiser's independent conclusions and the government's review of them. Not a price set by either party. These transactions provided the federal government with immediate control of strategic capacity that would otherwise take years to deliver. We believe these transactions also demonstrate the underlying value of the company's real estate portfolio. The sales further strengthen our already solid financial position and create meaningful balance sheet flexibility. Enabling us to return capital to shareholders while also evaluating opportunities to reinvest in and grow complementary businesses. Both organically and through disciplined strategic investment.

As we have previously disclosed in the press releases for the facilities that we have sold, we continue operating these 4 facilities under terms of the existing management contracts. However, contract terms may ultimately be due to the transfer of ownership to the federal government. And we continue to reflect the expectation of modified terms in our FY 2026 guidance. While we cannot predict when those modifications will happen, they will discuss the impact of our assumptions to both third quarter results and updated guidance. In addition to the asset sales completed to date, we continue to have discussions with ICE about the potential acquisition of additional CoreCivic detention facilities.

These discussions remain in preliminary stages we can provide no assurance as to the certainty or timing of any additional sales. However, we do believe additional facility acquisitions continue to be a key element of ICE's detention strategy as such acquisitions offer a unique opportunity for immediate government owned detention capacity. As previously announced, during the third quarter of 26, and prior to closing on the sale of the Appleton Correctional Facility, we announced a new contract award to manage the 1.6 thousand-bed facility that had been idled since 2010. We continue to expect this facility to have a minimal contribution to earnings in 2026 and expect to begin receiving detainees at this facility in the fourth quarter.

With activation estimated to be complete in the second quarter of 27. On the heels of the cash generating events that occurred during the quarter, we moved quickly to reduce our outstanding indebtedness and to increase the pace of our share repurchases. A portion of the net sale proceeds was used to repay in full the outstanding balance under our $575 million revolving credit facility, our incremental term loan, our initial term loan, and our senior notes that were scheduled to mature in 2027. Our $575 million revolving credit facility remains available to be redrawn.

Additionally, we launched a $500 million accelerated share repurchase agreement that enabled us to take immediate delivery of and retire 12.4 million shares of common stock. Contributing to a 13.8% reduction to weighted average diluted shares outstanding versus the prior year quarter. As a percentage of market capitalization, the ASR is among the largest ever executed. At the end of the quarter, we had $255.8 million of availability remaining under the company's existing share repurchase program.

Subsequent to the end of the third quarter 26, our Board of Directors approved a $500 million increase to our share repurchase authorization, bringing the total availability $755.8 million and giving us additional flexibility to deploy capital toward share buybacks under scoring our commitment to driving shareholder value and returns. In sum, it was an exceptionally robust quarter for value creating transactions, I am proud of the work our teams do every day to support our government partners and their ability to move with velocity when the partner requires it.

The timely execution of the facility sales and immediate thoughtful allocation of the proceeds showcases the intensity and professionalism the CoreCivic team is known for and I am appreciative of their efforts.

That growth reflects increased demand from our government partners as they rely on our staff to provide safe, humane housing, and services to those in their care.

Further, breaking down our federal mix, revenue from ICE increased $75.2 million or 34.8%, while revenue from the US Marshals Service decreased by $5.2 million versus the prior year quarter. Some of this decline is simply a shift in mix where ICE and the Marshals share a contract. We are particularly excited about the opportunities presented by CSP to better serve the pharmacy needs of our partners and believe their growing market reinforces our decision to purchase the company earlier this year. Populations from ICE and our care increased by approximately 8.1 thousand individuals or 80.1% from the beginning of 2025 through 09/30/2026 when we cared for 18.3 thousand individuals. Our average daily population increased by 3.3 thousand individuals in the third quarter of 26 from the second quarter of 26, Including a 1.16 thousand-person increase that incurred at the 5 facilities we have activated since the beginning of 2025.

Consistent with our internal forecast, ICE populations nationwide continue to rise in the second half of 26, approaching the record levels seen earlier this year after experiencing a decline in the second quarter of 26. David will review our population assumptions at a high level as reflected in our financial guidance. Populations at recently activated facilities have continued to increase driven by significant ICE demand. We continue to receive detainee populations at the 2.56 thousand-bed California City detention facility we signed a new contract effective 09/01/2025. And at our 2.16 thousand-bed Diamondback correctional facility, where we signed a new contract effective September thirtieth 25.

At the 1.03 thousand-bed Midwest Regional Reception Center, we began accepting detainees in March. As of September 30, we care for 451 individuals at this facility. We continue to maintain 4 idle corrections and detention, containing 5.5 thousand beds to meet federal or state needs. As a long term provider of detention and corrections capacity to government agencies across the country, our available capacity was built to meet established detention standards and ensures that we can offer humane, secure capacity in strategic locations.

While we do not control or influence the capacity needs of any government partner, we do ensure that the capacity in our system remains ready for activation should a need arise. Specifically, we believe our immediately available capacity continues to offer the most viable solution to meet ICE's anticipated continued needs. Their continued use of detention capacity is proactively supported by funding provided through the Homeland Security appropriations bill and the Secure America Act. This multiyear funding framework provides visibility into resources available for detention operations throughout its statutory authorization period and can be supplemented by annual appropriations to support these contracts going forward.

While future administration priorities, congressional policy decisions, and funding levels may evolve, We believe this funding framework positions ICE to maintain its detention capacity at or near current contracted level for the foreseeable future. As such, we continue to believe that our current share price implies a significant discount to the fair value. Based on updated guidance, our enterprise value to EBITDA multiple remains contracted, and we trade at a meaningful discount to our long term average. Accordingly, we view share repurchases as an attractive use of capital. The facility sales have also given us substantial financial flexibility and we will continue to evaluate opportunities to grow our business including disciplined strategic acquisitions.

While any potential transaction would need to be a strategic fit and compare favorably with repurchasing our own shares. We believe the continued prudent pursuit of high quality companies with growing markets like our acquisition of CSP earlier this year allows us to offer value to our shareholders for the long term. With that, I will turn the call over to David to discuss our third quarter financial results in more detail, our capital allocation activities and the assumptions underlying our updated 2026 financial guidance. David?

David Garfinkle: Thank you, Lucie Beth, and good morning, everyone. In the third quarter of 26, we generated GAAP EPS of $14.49 per diluted share and FFO per share of $0.84 Our GAAP results for the third quarter of 26 were significantly impacted by 5 discrete special items each as detailed in our earnings press release that favorably impacted GAAP net income by a net amount of $1.3 billion or $14 per diluted share, including most notably an after-tax $1.3 billion gain on the sale of 4 detention facilities. Excluding special items, adjusted EPS was $0.49 In the third quarter of 26, compared with $0.24 in the prior year quarter, an increase of 104%.

Normalized FFO per share was $0.75 per share, compared with $0.48 per share in the prior year quarter, an increase of 56%. And adjusted EBITDA was $117.8 million compared with $88.8 million in the third quarter of 25, an increase of 33%. Increase in adjusted EBITDA from the prior quarter resulted from the activation of 5 previously idled facilities under new management contracts with ICE. And the acquisition of Clinical Solutions Pharmacy or CSP on 04/01/2026. Our per share results were also favorably impacted by a 13.8% decrease in weighted average diluted shares outstanding as a result of our share repurchase program, including an accelerated share repurchase agreement ASR, implemented August 10, 26.

Our residential segment, which consists of the 64 correctional, detention, and reentry facilities we manage, generated 92.4% of our segment net operating income. Occupancy in the residential segment increased from 76.7% to 84% the highest occupancy level since the onset of the pandemic. Operating margin in the residential segment was relatively flat at 22.3%, versus 22.5% in the prior year quarter despite the increase in occupancy as the increase in occupancy was substantially attributable to the 5 previously idle facilities. Operating margins at the 4 facilities we were still activating during the third quarter were not yet contributing fully activated margin percentages.

These facilities, which were 72.7% occupied during the quarter, generated total EBITDA of $19.9 million operating margin of 8.7% in the third quarter of 26 up from 5.3% in the prior year quarter. As a reminder, the services segment consists of the delivery complementary services to the corrections industry, including pharmaceutical supplies and services through CSP, transportation through our subsidiary TransCore, and electronic monitoring and case management services as alternatives to incarceration through our subsidiary Recovery Monitoring Solutions. Net operating income in this segment increased $8.5 million from the prior year quarter, primarily due to the acquisition of CSP.

The Services segment generated 6.1% of our segment net operating income In the third quarter of 26, up from 0.9% in the prior year quarter due to the acquisition of CSP. Turning next to the balance sheet. As previously disclosed, during the third quarter, we completed the sales of 4 detention facilities containing 7.19 thousand beds to the Department of Homeland Security for a total gross sales price of $2.2 billion while retaining management of these facilities. Net proceeds were $1.6 billion after transaction costs and approximately $500 million in estimated federal and state income taxes including approximately $55 million still due in December.

We used a portion of the net proceeds to promptly repay over $700 million of debt during the third quarter, including the then outstanding balance of $270 million on our $575 million revolving credit facility that remains undrawn. $207.8 million of term loans under our bank credit facility and $238.5 million of principal amount of unsecured notes. As a result of these debt repayments, we expect to save over $40 million of annual interest expense. As mentioned, in August 2026, we entered into an ASR to purchase $500 million of our common stock.

We paid $500 million and received an initial delivery of 12.4 million shares, representing 80% of the payment based on the August 7 closing price of $32.27 per share. The final number of shares we will repurchase through the ASR will be based on the average of the daily volume weighted average price of our stock during the term of the ASR less a discount, with settlement expected to occur prior to the end of the second quarter of 2027. If we were to settle the ASR based on the average daily volume weighted average price through 10/31/2026, we would retire an additional 3.1 million shares at no additional cost.

We believe the ASR is the second largest ASR to be executed in the last 5 years, underscoring our commitment to returning capital to shareholders through share repurchases. As of September 30, excluding a non recourse mortgage, we now have more cash than debt with $1.1 billion of total liquidity available consisting of $547 million of cash on hand and $551 million of undrawn borrowing capacity under our revolving credit facility. This liquidity provides us with significant flexibility to our capital allocation strategy and growth plans. We have a track record of prudently allocating capital to create shareholder value.

Since we began the share repurchase program through the end of the second quarter of 26, we had repurchased 28.1 million shares of our stock at an aggregate cost of $444.2 million or $15.82 per share, amounting to 23% of the outstanding shares at the time of the authorization in May 2022. On 07/01/2025, we completed the acquisition of the Farmville Detention Center for a purchase price of $71.3 million And on 04/01/2026, we completed the acquisition of CSP for an initial purchase price of $148 million. These acquisitions were completed at a blended multiple of under 6x.

As previously mentioned, we reacted quickly to an opportunity to create additional shareholder value through the sales of 4 detention facilities and separate transactions generating gross proceeds of $2.2 billion during the third quarter of 26. And as mentioned, we rapidly deployed a portion of the net proceeds to repay over $700 million of debt within the same quarter and entered into the $500 million ASR agreement.

Earlier this week, the Board of Directors authorized an increase to our existing share repurchase program under which we may purchase up to an additional $500 million in shares of our common stock increasing the total repurchase authorization to $1.7 billion Including the additional authorization, we have $755.8 million of repurchase authorization available under the share repurchase program. Given our substantial liquidity and our belief that our shares remain undervalued, we expect share repurchases to remain a priority for capital deployment. We expect to repurchase shares using the most expedient available methods subject to applicable legal requirements restrictions under our existing ASR agreement, and trading window limitations.

We will also continue to evaluate strategic acquisitions in attractive growth markets where we can have a competitive advantage in the market, but any acquisition would need to be strategically compelling, financially attractive, and compare favorably with repurchasing our own shares and other capital allocation alternatives. Moving lastly to a discussion of our updated 2026 financial guidance, we expect to generate diluted EPS of $15.42 to $15.48 which includes the impact of the special items and adjusted diluted EPS, which excludes the special items, of $1.74 to $1.80 up from $1.64 to $1.73 in our August 10 guidance.

We expect to generate normalized FFO per share of $2.78 to $2.85 up from $2.68 to $2.77 We expect adjusted EBITDA of $442.5 million to $445.5 million compared with $434.5 million to $439.5 million Our updated guidance reflects stable residential populations for the rest of the year, except the facilities we are activating where we expect populations to continue to increase. Our updated guidance contemplates additional G&A expense in Q4 associated with the executive leadership change and incremental technology expenses to assess opportunities to streamline operations and improve outcomes for residents in our care.

Our updated guidance carries forward the Q3 beat on facility margins against our forecast, a substantial portion of which was attributable to modifications contemplated to our contracts at the 4 facilities we sold during the third quarter, which has not yet occurred. The range of our updated guidance incorporates our best estimate of the financial impact of the change in ownership. Finally, we expect our annual effective tax rate to be 27% to 28%, slightly higher related to the asset sales during the third quarter as well as higher nondeductible expenses. In addition to the facility sales completed to date, we have been in discussions with ICE about the potential acquisition of additional detention facilities from us.

These discussions are in preliminary stages and we can provide no assurance that any additional sales will occur. Our updated guidance does not include the impact of any potential additional facility sales. We plan to spend $65 million to $75 million on maintenance capital expenditures during 2026 and $12 million for other capital expenditures down $3 million from our prior guidance. Our 2026 forecast also includes $35 million to $40 million for capital expenditures associated with previously idled facilities we are activating and for additional potential facility activations, unchanged from our prior guidance.

We expect adjusted funds from operations or AFFO which we consider a proxy for our cash flow available for capital allocation decisions such as share repurchases and growth CapEx such as acquisitions and facility activations, to range from $260.3 million to $272.3 million for 2026. Although we do not expect to need any meaningful capital expenditures to activate additional idle facilities in 2027, we currently expect some incremental capital expenditures in 2027 to expand the distribution capability of CSP to meet growing market demands for our pharmaceutical services and incremental technology investments to implement opportunities to streamline operations and improve outcomes for residents in our care.

The earn out payment for CSP will also be due next year which we currently estimate to be $29.5 million. We are currently preparing our 2027 budget and expect to provide full year 2027 guidance in connection with our fourth quarter earnings release in February including further detail on our capital expenditures in 2027. That guidance will also reflect estimated financial impact of the change in ownership of the facilities we have sold which may be known by then. Major tech stocks plunge as US midterms add to fears of AI slowdown

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Hundreds of billions of dollars in market value has been wiped from major tech firms in the US and Asia in a stock market rout after the US midterm elections sparked concerns of a slowdown in artificial intelligence development.

Shares in firms across the tech and AI sectors plummeted following Democratic wins after election campaigns run on slowing AI.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq composite index closed down by more than 5.5%, losing an estimated $2.5tn (Â£1.9tn) in market value, according to Dow Jones data, while other key US stock indexes also saw big losses in their worst days since 2025.

The sell-off comes on top of broader concerns about whether the huge amounts being spent on AI will pay off.

In Asia, tech-heavy indexes fell sharply on Friday morning as firms that make the computer chips and other equipment that power AI saw their shares slide.

The Nikkei 225 in Japan was 6% lower in early trading as shares in memory chip maker Kioxia lost over 12.6% and rival Tokyo Electron slipped by 9.5%.

South Korean chipmaker SK Hynix fell by more than 11.5%, while Samsung was down by nearly 10%.

The sell-off in Seoul sent the Kospi index plunging more than 8.5%, triggering a so-called circuit breaker, a mechanism that temporarily halts trading to curb panic selling.

Taiwan's Taiex dropped by more than 8.3%, with chipmaking giant TSMC falling by nearly 8.5%.

It marks a "continuation of worries" over the level of spending on AI infrastructure, said MarketWatch chips analyst Britney Nguyen.

Democrats in races across the US campaigned to slow the growth of data centres and AI, and there were dozens of measures on ballots around the country aimed at restricting the development of the hubs crucial for powering the technology.

They won both the US Senate and House of Representatives in Tuesday's midterms, taking control of Congress for the rest of President Donald Trump's presidency.

Texas' Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned heavily against data centres, pledging a moratorium on new construction in the sector.

Live: What can Democrats do with control of Congress? We answer your US midterms questions

Food, fuel and taxes â can the Democrats actually make life cheaper?

Boebert, billionaires' tax and other midterms races still to watch

In a social media post, Trump blamed the slide on his political opponents.

"The Stock Market suffered one of the Worst Days in History based on the results of the Election," he said.

"Remember, it wasn't Republicans, it was Dumocrats. Do not fear, however, we will soon, one way or the other, be BIGGER AND STRONGER THAN EVER BEFORE. MAKE AMERICA GREAT AGAIN!"

Some Democrats have argued that AI has grown too big without any safeguards or oversight and have promised, with their new power in Congress, to change that.

"Americans don't want Big Tech oligarchs wrecking the environment, gutting jobs and threatening humanity's future. NATIONAL DATA CENTER MORATORIUM NOW," Democrat Senator Bernie Sanders said in a post on X on Thursday.

Shares in companies heavily exposed to AI data centres, including crucial chipmakers and semiconductor manufacturers, fell sharply on Thursday, highlighting investor concerns that had already been growing.

Nvidia shares slid more than 9%, knocking more than $400bn off the firm's market value.

Other chipmakers also suffered, with AMD and Micron both dropping by more than 17%. Intel shares also dropped, down 10.3%, while memory card maker SanDisk plunged more than 22%.

The Philadelphia Semiconductor Index, which tracks the performance of 30 of the largest companies involved in semiconductor production, fell by more than 12%.

Shares in Elon Musk's companies SpaceX and Tesla also plunged, falling 8% and nearly 11% respectively and shaving more than $300bn off the firms' market value on Thursday.

Musk had spent nearly $450m backing Republican campaigns, including at least $45m in support of Ken Paxton for the Texas senate seat, but Texas was turned blue in the Democratic tsunami on Tuesday night.

In total, the so-called magnificent seven tech companies, including Apple, Nvidia and Tesla, have shed a trillion dollars in value in a single trading day.

The stock falls also hit other major US stock indexes. The S&P 500 index fell by 4.4% by the end of trading, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average erased gains made on Wednesday to close down more than 3.4%.

The Nasdaq Composite fall was so large it is now in what market watchers call correction territory, which is where an index or company value falls at least 10% below its recent high.

A backlash over data centres is another threat to the AI juggernaut

Big, noisy and coming to a town near you - why the data centre backlash is growing

Susannah Streeter, chief investment strategist at Wealth Club, said the steep sell-off on the Nasdaq and the S&P 500 was sending shockwaves into other markets.

"Fresh panic over the AI trade has hit Wall Street hard," she said.

"Fears that the vast sums poured into AI infrastructure may not deliver the returns investors have been banking on are now colliding with the prospect of a sharp slowdown in data centre construction."

Greater scrutiny of data centre approvals, and their impact on the environment and electricity bills could delay projects and raise costs across the industry, said Charu Chanana, Chief Investment Strategist at Saxo.

While both political parties are keen to ensure the US' dominance in the technology, "there is a difference between supporting AI development and allowing infrastructure to expand at any cost or pace," she said.

Some tech leaders have said they were caught off guard by the backlash against data centres in the midterms.

OpenAI president and co-founder Greg Brockman said he believed everyone had been surprised by the reaction to data centres, and more work needed to be done to ensure communities were on board.

"A lot of our shift over the course of this year has really been to realise that from San Francisco trying to build infrastructure in Texas, Georgia, and other places, you can't just tweet and say: 'This is going to be good.' You need to be in those communities," Brockman said at the WSJ Tech conference in Napa, California on Wednesday evening.

Meta's president Dina Powell McCormick said that the industry needed to put more effort into selling AI as a good idea.

"I think we need to double and triple down on telling the story," she told the WSJ conference.

Alongside the immediate impacts of the US mid-term elections, some investors have become concerned that AI shares could be in a bubble that is due a correction.

Micron Technology vs. Western Digital: Which Tech Stock Is the Better Buy for 2027?

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Both memory and storage stocks are riding the AI data boom, but one may be much better prepared for the next downturn.

The data-heavy world of artificial intelligence (AI) creates massive demand for storage and memory. This shift forces investors to choose between Micron Technology (MU -17.46%) and Western Digital (WDC -17.36%) as they eye long-term gains.

Micron specializes in DRAM and NAND memory used in everything from mobile phones to high-end servers. Western Digital focuses on hard disk drives and flash solutions, specifically targeting the cloud infrastructure sector. Both are booming right now, but the better buy may depend on which business holds up when the boom cools.

As one of the leading semiconductor stocks, Micron Technology designs and manufactures essential memory chips like DRAM and NAND. It serves a wide range of markets including data centers, mobile devices, and the automotive sector. In fiscal 2026 (which ended in September), over half of total revenue came from its top 10 customers, and such customer concentration adds a layer of risk to the business.

Fiscal 2026 revenue reached $133 billion, representing massive year-over-year growth of approximately 256%. Net income grew 895% to $85 billion. This reflects a significant increase from its performance in recent years as memory prices soared due to limited supply and high demand.

As for financial health, the company has virtually no debt. Its current ratio, which measures the ability to cover short-term obligations with current assets, sits at a healthy 3.3x. Micron also generated nearly $59 billion in free cash flow, which is the cash left over after paying for its operations and capital equipment.

Western Digital provides storage solutions for enterprise clients and hyperscale cloud providers. The cloud end market is vital for the company, accounting for nearly 89% of net revenue in its latest fiscal year.

Trailing-12-month (TTM) revenue through the October quarter reached $14.5 billion, representing growth of 43% over the previous year. Net income reached $10 billion. Western Digital is benefiting from the same dynamic as Micron, with limited supply of storage products driving a favorable pricing environment.

Western Digital has a debt-to-equity ratio of roughly 0.1x. The company holds a current ratio of nearly 1.4x and generated approximately $4.3 billion in free cash flow over the past year.

Both companies face risks from the extreme price volatility often found in the memory and data storage market. These stocks could also fall if anything slowed data center construction, such as new regulations or other restrictions, or if AI infrastructure spending slowed.

Micron competes against major rivals like Samsung Electronics and SK Hynix. Additionally, geopolitical tensions and trade restrictions involving China could limit the company's ability to sell to key infrastructure operators. Western Digital competes against Seagate and Toshiba in the storage market.

Micron trades at a lower Forward P/E, measuring price against future earnings estimates, and a lower P/S ratio, comparing its share price to revenue over the past twelve months.

Valuation metrics include those sourced from Financial Modeling Prep (FMP) and may differ from those of other data providers.

The better buy is not the stock with the highest upside, but the one that will be more resilient during the next downturn. Western Digital is better positioned to handle a stall in data center spending, and it's the stock I would buy right now.

Micron has seen its revenue and margins soar over the last year, but that sharp swing in its results also shows how vulnerable it is to a sudden dip in memory prices. While it is signing long-term customer agreements to help smooth out future cycles, it won't cover all of its revenue. It is still vulnerable to a downturn in the memory market.

Western Digital is exposed to similar booms and busts as Micron. However, demand for storage products tends to be more stable than memory. This is because data centers will continue to accumulate more data even as spending on new server orders slows.

This is evident in each company's past gross margin. Over the last 10 years, Micron's gross margin has swung from negative 9% in fiscal 2023 to as high as 81% in fiscal 2026. Western Digital's lowest gross margin over the same period was 22% in fiscal 2023, and its highest was 49% in fiscal 2026.

Western Digital's greater resilience is also why the stock trades at a higher P/E. Investors are paying a lower P/E multiple for Micron because they expect greater volatility in its financial results if memory prices fall.

At this point in the cycle, I would rather buy Western Digital heading into 2027.
