Upcoming Paramount+ Shows Reportedly Eying Move To HBO Max

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The future of Paramount+ shows is getting clearer.

With Paramount's acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery, one of the biggest questions was what would happen to the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services. The post-merger company, which is now known as Skydance, appointed HBO's Casey Bloys as the Co-Chair and Chief Content Officer of Skydance Direct-to-Consumer, in which he is responsible for overseeing both Paramount+ and HBO Max, and co-CEO David Ellison has discussed plan to eventually merge the two services.

A new report from Deadline now shares that while no official decisions have been made yet, the possibility is being discussed of moving Paramount+'s upcoming drama series to HBO Max. Potential candidates include Nicole Kidman and Elle Fanning's legal thriller Discretion, the 9/11 first responders show The Best of Us led by Succession's Jeremy Strong, and other projects that are in the midst of waiting for an official greenlight.

Conversations about this move have reportedly reached the highest levels of Skydance, but all Paramount+ shows are staying on the platform for now as talks continue to progress. Meanwhile, Francesca Orsi, who was promoted from EVP to President of HBO Drama Series and Films, is working with Bloys to decide which drama projects belong on HBO.

As part of reviewing the Paramount+ slate with Sarah Aubrey, Head of Paramount+, Max Originals and International Programming, Orsi allegedly may also be tasked with looking at which Paramount+ series feel aligned with HBO, along with working on the select shows that stay on Paramount+, especially if they are akin to HBO's prestige dramas.

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

Sign in to claim your place on the leaderboard!

While the new streaming structure is still being worked out, moving upcoming Paramount+ shows like Discretion and The Best of Us to HBO Max could potentially expedite plans to merge the platforms together. From Kidman's time on Big Little Lies and The Undoing and Strong playing Kendall Roy in Succession, the stars of these series have plenty of Emmy Award-winning experience with HBO, making their new prestige dramas a natural fit.

This may be the trajectory for Paramount+'s upcoming shows, but it does not answer what will happen to the ones that already exist. From Landman to The Madison, there is no shortage of ongoing Taylor Sheridan shows on Paramount+, but it is unclear whether they will also move to HBO Max, especially before his NBCUniversal television deal begins in 2029. There's also the matter of where unscripted shows will go, including the many reality ones that also air on CBS, such as Big Brother, Survivor, and The Amazing Race.

If all Paramount+ content eventually shifts to HBO Max, there is additionally the matter of whether Skydance's streaming service will just be called HBO Max, if it will combine names, or if there will be a complete rebranding. Before the merger, HBO's streaming platform already underwent many name changes, including HBO Go, HBO Now, HBO Max, Max, and eventually back to HBO Max. Another major and more pressing question is what the subscription prices will look like if the two services and all their movies and shows are fully merged.

The 10 Most Elite Movies of 2026 So Far, Ranked

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Each year, doomsayers are ready to pronounce cinema dead. Since the pandemic, 2026 has been the most triumphant and encouraging year for the medium, particularly regarding theatrical exhibition, with a handful of unexpected hits dominating the zeitgeist. This year has proven that you don't need a giant superhero epic to draw people to theaters, as everything from romantic horror movies to A24 documentaries have brought casual audiences to their local multiplex. 2026 has seen many of the highest-grossing movies of all time and a collection of gems and soon-to-be classics, including this list of the ten best of the year so far, ranked.

In a year filled with unexpected plot twists and revelations, The Drama took the cake as the most shocking cinematic confession of the year. The Drama, a hilariously macabre and provocative black comedy-drama by Kristoffer Borgli, was the first of three collaborations by Zendaya and Robert Pattinson, and their most dynamic on-screen partnership. The film touched third rails, but it wasn't edgy for the sole purpose of shock value.

On the precipice of their wedding, Emma Harwood (Zendaya) discloses a horrifying and unsettling secret from her youth, which causes her fiancé, Charlie Thompson (Pattinson), to spiral, reckoning with his decision to devote his life to this woman. A film that's often hard to watch due to its cringe-inducing social faux pas and uncomfortable confrontations with society's most taboo subjects, The Drama is nonetheless fascinated by the politics of romantic relationships, as well as the conflicts that arise when people give themselves over to each other. There's a real heart to the romance between Emma and Charlie that balances out Borgli's pitch-black humor and dissection of American values. The Norwegian filmmaker asks: are Americans too prude or too crass? Either way, the combination is destructive.

A movie that's slipped through the cracks during the busy fall lineup, Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother is the film that should keep Disney up at night. After failing to get their Blade movie off the ground as part of the Marvel Cinematic Universe, director Bassam Tariq and star Mahershala Ali finally dropped out and teamed up to make Your Mother Your Mother Your Mother, a bold action-thriller that's both rip-roaring and thoughtful as a reflection of Muslim identity.

Lesson learned for Hollywood: don't overthink the project and give Tariq and Ali enough resources to make a crowd-pleasing and socially conscious film, and you should expect greatness. The film was a glorious return for the two-time Oscar winner of Moonlight and Green Book, who gives perhaps his signature movie-star turn as Latif, an ex-military hitman who carries out jobs for a religious leader caught in the crossfire of Islamic and Christian forces while raising his kids as a single dad. Your Mother's fight choreography is as good, if not superior, to, anything you'll see in a major blockbuster, and its combat is a fresh evolution after years of John Wick clones. Tariq tells a deeply personal and urgent story of a conflicted assassin who struggles with his faith.

Trust us, accept Oliva Wilde's invitation to this raunchy, scintillating, and candid dramedy about all the things most people aren't ready to talk about. The Invite, a single-location, adult-oriented character study about two couples discussing sex, career ambition, and familial responsibility versus individualism, is the kind of movie that used to release regularly from the studio system. Now that they are a rare breed in 2026, films like The Invite feel like a festive celebration, but maybe not as freaky as whatever is going on in this film's apartment.

When an on-the-rocks, tense married couple, Angela (Wilde) and Joe (Seth Rogen), invite their enigmatic upstairs neighbors, the quirky and feisty couple, Pina (Penélope Cruz) and Hawk (Edward Norton), they reconsider their entire lives and the status of their relationship, as truths unspool and hard feelings are laid out on the table during this wild dinner party. A stylish hybrid between a raunchy comedy and an intimate chamber drama, The Invite appeals to a wide audience, even with its commentary that's designed to reflect on your own discomfort. Wilde's precise behind-the-camera vision gives the film the edge of a psychological thriller, as the canted angles and intricate blocking create an uncanny vibe and awkward tension, a sentiment that everyone who's ever been paired up with an odd couple can relate to.

2026's first tentpole release, Project Hail Mary continues to linger in film culture since its release in March. Based on an Andy Weir novel and directed by Phil Lord and Chris Miller after a 12-year hiatus from the director's chair, the film was an inspired box office smash hit, as it offered a glimmer of hope for audiences that star-driven, non-franchise event movies can still thrive. The year was a big winner for Ryan Gosling and hand-crafted, practical effects.

A charismatic screen icon, a rock puppet, and an outer-space journey about going home and teamwork. What more else could you ask for? Project Hail Mary reinvigorated the traditional four-quadrant blockbuster after years of studios neglecting so many of its creative principles. The film, despite its PG-13 rating, is something that people of ostensibly all ages can enjoy, and its melancholic streak and sci-fi wizardry keep the cinephiles in the theater locked in. Dr. Ryland Grace (Gosling) is the idyllic movie protagonist—amiable, courageous, and sometimes naive. It is the part of a lifetime for Gosling, who has become a master of bumbling humor and dramatic sincerity, and Lord and Miller let their pop culture obsessions soar to the moon.

This is certainly a controversial pick, to put it mildly. Even when accepting some of the writing shortcomings and plot dynamics, Disclosure Day is yet another awe-inspiring, luminous vision by the legendary Steven Spielberg. One of 2026's most divisive movies left a bad taste in some viewers' mouths, but for those who were on its wavelength, the film's majestic scope and hyper-earnest tone made this feel like the most important cinematic statement of the year.

Featuring a career-best performance by Emily Blunt and Spielberg operating behind the camera as if he hasn't aged, time will be kind to this misunderstood achievement.

Disclosure Day, which saw Spielberg returning to the alien subgenre, is a mega-sized blockbuster/auteurist expression of many layers. A conspiracy thriller, sci-fi mystery, and existential drama wrapped into one, the film was criticized for being a retrograde and out-of-touch depiction of contemporary America and our relationship to government secrets coming from a septuagenarian filmmaker, but Spielberg's optimism is magnificently used to wrestle with contemporary cynicism. Disclosure Day posits a world where people's skepticism and ulterior motives shatter in the face of something truly profound. The film, filled with intense chase sequences and moving character moments, is far stranger and thematically inscrutable than Spielberg's usual fare. Featuring a career-best performance by Emily Blunt and Spielberg operating behind the camera as if he hasn't aged, time will be kind to this misunderstood achievement.

Why don't you have a seat over there? Primetime, starring Robert Pattinson (arguably the MVP of 2026) as To Catch a Predator's Chris Hansen, is an essential watch and one of the most striking films of the year. The first narrative feature by documentarian Lance Oppenheim, this surrealistic, unconventional biopic about one of the 2000s most polarizing media figures is a dazzling and off-putting morality fable about the sinister nature of performance art and vigilante justice.

Now 20 years removed from the height of To Catch a Predator's popularity on NBC, Primetime reflects on this bizarre and unruly era for culture from a contemporary lens. Oppenheim dives into this period by mimicking the visual and tonal style of an America obsessed with trashy reality TV, resulting in a hypnotic aesthetic that pushes the medium to new realms. Even if he has the moral high ground relative to the sexual predators that the show is incriminating, it takes a warped mind to make a living off turning federal prosecutions into performance art for millions at home on a Thursday night. Primetime, carried by Pattinson in the performance of the year, captures the world through Hansen's obsessions and unseemly desires. The film speaks to present-day perils by depicting an America where our heroes are on television, glorifying the hunt for sex offenders as a circus act, and where the media is our only outlet for justice.

One of the many movies of 2026 that needed to be seen to be believed, Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie is unlike anything audiences have ever seen on the big screen. Explaining Matt Johnson's style and the premise of the Canadian web series that the film is based on, which has no affiliation with the famous grunge band, is a tall task, but anyone with a good sense of humor and fervent passion for creative companionship will adore all the madness that's under the hood of this time-traveling music mockumentary.

For Matt Johnson and series co-creator Jay McCarrol, playing themselves in the film, the goal is to land a gig at the Rivoli nightclub in Toronto, but when inadvertently traveling back in time to 2008, they stumble into a chance to attain fame that will carry them to the present day. Nirvanna the Band the Show the Movie's audacious concept doesn't disappoint in the final product, as Johnson and McCarrol put on the most electric, hilarious, and sweeping adventure that satirizes showbiz, movie history, and culture, best exemplified in the initial arrival in 2008, before the fall from grace of various canceled celebrities and societal rejections of insensitive humor. With the right crowd, Nirvanna the Band plays like a rock concert, with every joke and suspenseful, jaw-dropping set piece leaving the room completely enraptured.

Many inventive and groundbreaking movies entered the cultural zeitgeist in 2026, but there are still a handful of smaller, arthouse films that likely slipped past your radar. Look no further than Blue Heron, another crowning achievement of Canadian cinema and the announcement of a new visionary, Sophy Romvari. This personal, gut-wrenching, and transcendent portrait of grief and the erosion of memory is sure to leave you speechless and exploring your own soul.

The meaning of life, family, and distant memories are beautifully reflected in Romvari's debut feature film, which follows a filmmaker, Sasha (Eylul Guven as a child and Amy Zimmer as an adult), recalling a summer in the '90s when her brother's disruptive behavior, the result of untreated mental illness, caused a strain in her household in Vancouver. As a family drama, Blue Heron is palpably sensitive, sparking an array of Proustian memories where the minuscule incidents trigger the most harrowing sensations. The film's somber register evokes Romvari and her avatar trying to repress emotions, as the director is still trying to grasp why these memories continue to linger. This meta-textual, deconstructionist story about the stories we tell ourselves to make sense of the past shifts into gear in its second half, where the audience wrestles with how we treat male mental illness and the mind's constant longing to rewrite history.

In terms of scope, anticipation, critical discussion, and cultural omnipresence, The Odyssey will undoubtedly stand as the cinematic event of the year, if not the decade. Christopher Nolan, with yet another critically acclaimed, auteurist blockbuster under his belt, proved himself to be the most viable name in Hollywood, more than any other living movie star. His biggest swing yet gave you everything you wanted out of a major motion picture extravaganza ripe with humanity, grace, and pathos.

Rounded out by an absurdly stacked cast, including Matt Damon as Odysseus, Tom Holland as Telemachus, and Anne Hathaway as Penelope, The Odyssey was something everyone had to be part of in some way. Each star, even the surprise scene-stealers like Samantha Morton and Elliot Page, helped craft the modern-day classical epic. Nolan, who miraculously shot the entire movie on IMAX film, painted with his broadest canvas yet, but he never lost his control, momentum, and probing psychology that have made him the signature visionary of his generation. A stunningly intimate epic about trauma, destiny, and legacy, The Odyssey grounded the overwhelming scale of Homer's epic poem without minimizing the all-encompassing weight of the text. Nolan, who brought a fresh take on an ancient story, is perhaps the quintessential storyteller of our modern world.

Rarely do you see a feature film garner this much online buzz and sheer curiosity, let alone a documentary. However, anyone with an ounce of awareness of Nathan Fielder knew that You Can See Everything, a film we didn't even know existed two months ago, would break the confines of the documentary form. Chronicling convicted Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes before she's sent to prison and much more, the film, co-directed by Lance Oppenheim, showed the glowing potential for cinema in the 2020s as a form-breaking artistic expression about everything and nothing at the same time, which is what living in the present day feels like.

No logline does You Can See Everything justice, as Fielder and Oppenheim's cinéma vérité coverage of Holmes and her partner Billy Evans evolves into a bizarre, unsettling, and hypnotic dissection of filmmaking, egoism, performance art, and parasocial relationships. For avid fans of Nathan for You and The Rehearsal, the shocking box office hit and potential awards player upends the Fielder we've grown to love or despise, as his deadpan awkwardness shatters in the face of the curious case of Elizabeth Holmes, who develops an uncanny relationship with her documentarian as Fielder attempts to read and connect with her. The film, which features a second-half shift in perspective that's becoming harder and harder to keep from being spoiled, reflects how some people are born incapable of truth and basic communication. You Can See Everything is the cultural text of the year, as its depiction of morally grey tech moguls and constant nonfiction image-making taps directly into our hearts.

One in 15 adults has experienced AI-generated image-based abuse, study finds

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Around one in 15 adults has experienced AI-generated image-based abuse, according to a new study. The general-population survey of more than 7,200 adults in the United States, the United Kingdom and Australia found 7% had experienced at least one form of AI-generated image-based abuse since turning 18.

Love Is Blind's Stew Debuts Beard Transformation After Popular Demand

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Love Is Blind star Scott "Stew" Stewart showed off a new look after Netflix fans campaigned for him to grow a beard following his short-lived engagement with Jacqueline "Jax" Guelli.

Scott "Stew" Stewart debuted a blinding glow-up.

The Love Is Blind star took the internet's advice by growing a beard to enhance his facial features.

"The sweet smell of victory," the 34-year-old captioned a TikTok showing off his suave new stubble, using audio of Michael Scott's famed line in The Office, "Well, well, well, how the turn tables turn."

And viewers were delighted to discover Stew's dapper new facial hair ahead of the season 11 reunion, set to air Nov. 8, after fan pages, hairstylists and med spas circulated images online that reimagined the reality star with a brawny beard to accentuate his jawline while complementing his eyes.

"The beard has arrived," one user wrote, to which Stew responded, “The people have spoken!!!"

And when another fan added, "Keep growing it out trust us," he quipped, "I never doubted you."

Stew is taking any styling tips in stride, responding to a user demanding he grow his beard longer by writing alongside a crying emoji, "Noted for the next one."

Stew's appearance first became a topic of conversation after his fiancé Jacqueline "Jax" Guelli called off their engagement seconds after their first face-to-face meeting despite building a connection behind a wall. When the engineer told Jax that he'd gained a reputation in the pods for resembling a Kennedy, she quipped, "More like Mitt Romney."

But the 31-year-old later clarified that she didn't ghost Stew on a whim despite how it appeared on the show.

"I just lost control of my emotions, and all I knew was to go home," Jax told E! News. "Truly it had nothing to do with him as a person."

She admitted that her cold feet began with the proposal in the pods, adding, "I was like, 'That didn't feel right.'"

Still, Jax moved forward with the relationship, hoping their in-person connection would take her by surprise.

"I wanted it to be like an aha moment, like, 'OK, now all is well. We have real time chemistry and connection,'" she explained. "It just was not there for me. And unfortunately, I panicked and I left."

Since then, the former couple has discussed the mishap "many times," the skincare founder said, including when she sent Stew a DM after leaving the show.

"It was super sincere," Stew told E! News. "She explained that it wasn't about me. It was about just the emotional overload of the previous couple weeks."

Read on for more behind-the-scenes of Love Is Blind, straight from the pod squad.

1. Are contestants paid to be on Love Is Blind? Former star Matthew Duliba said they are paid $5,000 to appear on the pod period of the show.

The real money is made in sponsorships afterward, as season two alums Deepti Vempati and Natalie Lee said they each made about $500,000 as influencers in under two years after their episodes aired in 2022.

2. Where do the cast members sleep while in the pods? According to Netflix, the contestants slept on cot beds in trailers next to the lounges during season one, but for season two they upgraded to hotel rooms to be more comfortable. They're escorted to and from their rooms by staff so they can't secretly see anyone.

"You're actually at a hotel," season four's Micah Lussier shared on TikTok in 2025. "That's where you're staying, and then the pods that you see is a set, so think of a big studio, and then the bathrooms are outside in trailers."

3. The reality stars don't get access to their phones starting in the pods until after they're back from their tropical vacation portion of filming.

Shayne Jansen shared of their vacations, "You don't have your phone or anything on you."

4. The pods portion of the experiment lasts about 10 days, with 40-50 hopeful singles serving as the initial cast. But over the course of the process, some singles are let go.

"There was a certain point where we did a whittling down of people just to focus internally on the people who were really connecting, to allow them more time," Love Is Blind creator Chris Coelen told E! News. "We ended up having maybe 20-25 people in the pods at that point."

5. The first dates between all of the singles were set up speed-dating style rotation, with everyone spending eight to 10 minutes with each person of the opposite sex. From then on, there were no limitations put on who you could talk to, when you could talk to them and for how long, as long as a production intermediary set it up as they were unable to communicate with each other outside of the pods.

6. According to Sal Perez from season two, the singles are given "talking points" and "questions" before heading into their dates, but they don't have to use them.

"There were kind of topics for the day," Kyle Abrams added in an interview with E! News. "It was good if there was a lull in conversation, you just refer to the little notebook and you can drum up conversation."

That being said, the show is far from scripted. As contestant Kelly Chase told Business Insider, "Although it might sound unbelievable (trust me, it was hard to believe myself), the show wasn't scripted for the cast."

7. As for what they eat on set? Kelly said that was one of the aspects of filming she enjoyed most, explaining that contestants are offered a wide range of options while in the pods.

"During the first 10 days of filming," she said, "we could choose from catered food that included options like grilled fish, chicken, steak, vegetables, pasta, rice, and fruit."

8. All the proposals happen on the same day, though some are shown earlier in the episodes to avoid watching them all back-to-back.

“Everyone gets engaged on that one day. And then the next day is the big reveal day," Kelly said, per Cosmo. "And then the couples separate and go back through their own door. You meet them [your partner] for 15 minutes—the person you just got engaged to—and then you don’t see them again for a few days. It’s crazy."

9. Where do the engagement rings come from?

"We supply them with the rings, and at that point, those rings are theirs," the creator told Entertainment Weekly in 2025. "They never have to give them back if things don't work out. They're their rings. And if they want to say that they paid for them, then that's up to them. They can say whatever they want."

10. Hosts Vanessa Lachey and Nick Lachey are no longer allowed to be at the reveals.

"We all used to be at the reveal," Vanessa told Parade in 2025. "So when they would come out on the red carpet, it was me, it was Nick, we had like the glam girls there, we had costume design, we had audio. We had everybody standing there."

But it ended up being "probably 50 people" in the room, which detracted from the romance.

"It got to a point where it got too loud," Vanessa added. "After that, they were like, 'No more people at the reveal. Just the cameras and the couple. This is their moment.'"

11. Hoping to see a few proposals, producers were not expecting more than five couples to get engaged in season one. So when two other couples (in addition to the six the show chose to follow) also ended the pod portion with an engagement, producers ultimately couldn't continue filming their journey.

"We felt like we wanted to tell diverse stories frankly," Coelen reasoned. "We weren't sure where the stories were going to go, everybody felt like they truly found the person they wanted to spend their life with and we had an abundance of story...we just didn't have time in the show to follow them, which is incredible to me."

12. One of those engaged couples was Rory Newbrough, who was often seen giving advice to the other men, and Danielle Drouin. They spent a week together in Miami as the other engaged couples went to Mexico to continue filming. But soon after they returned to Atlanta, they had a "very amicable" breakup when Danielle realized she still had feelings for another cast member, Matt Thomas. "She ended up wanting to pursue that," Rory explained to People. Danielle and Matt ultimately didn't last either.

13. The other unaired season one engagement was between Westley Baer, who was briefly shown discussing dating as a shorter man, and Lexie Skipper. Despite not continuing with the show, they dated for three months back in Atlanta. But Westley revealed to People that his career change caused their split. "I made a crazy decision to sell everything I owned, and I moved to Asia," he said.

Two season two couples—Caitlin McKee and Joey Miller and Kara Williams and Jason Beaumont—also got engaged, but didn't have their journeys filmed.

14. Are constants allowed to talk when there's not cameras around?

"In the pod stage, the answer is no," Micah said. "There's always cameras rolling in those pods. And when you're in the lounge, they don't want people talking if there's not cameras rolling, so it will be quiet on set. And that is because if you're talking about stuff, they can miss part of the storyline."

She said the experience in Mexico is similar.

"You won't fly together, you won't do anything off camera until they leave you alone in that room that first night," she said. "So, that's the first time that you get a talk off-camera."

But when they go home, there's more freedom to speak without being filmed.

"You have scheduled filming days, like every other day," she explained. "So of course, you get a ton of time off camera to get to know each other without them in your face."

"They pay for the dress," Micah said of the production team. "I remember when we went there were some dresses that were included and part of the budget, and we could choose from those. And there's a lot to choose from."

However, they also "had the option" to "upgrade" to a more expensive dress that they paid for. The season four star added, "To my knowledge, no one ever did that."

16. As for who paid for the weddings, a Netflix spokesperson revealed to Women's Health that production "supplies some of the basics, but because these are their real weddings, it's up to them as to how to spend their money." So the couples likely did have to cover some expense if they went over budget.

17. Despite some skepticism from viewers, the marriages are legit, with season one's Lauren Speed telling Refinery29, "This is a real legally binding marriage. This is not for TV. This is our life."

18. It's not random who goes first on the alter saying "I do" or "I don't," with Micah saying the production crew determines who goes first.

"It's not the officiant who decides," Micah revealed. "In my opinion, they will ask whoever they think is more likely to say 'yes' to go first, because then they get a two [person] answer."

Otherwise, she noted, "you don't have that chance of seeing the first person say 'yes' and having that shock."

19. So were the cast members obligated to make it to the altar? According to Coelen, they were not. "They certainly could choose to do whatever they wanted to do," he told E! News of the wedding day decision-making process. "Again, being there on the wedding day, I personally, knowing these people and their stories, I didn't know what was going to happen."

20. Then why do people wait until the altar to decide if they're going to say yes or no? While contestants aren't required to go through with the wedding day, there's a reason many do, even if they ultimately say no.

"In my experience, it wasn't like you have to get to the altar, like you must get to the altar," Micah shared on TikTok. "It was moreso being a part of the entire experience. The whole point of Love Is Blind is It's an experiment. And you wouldn't know if it's going to work or not unless you do the whole thing. So that was the thought process there."

21. Why do so few couples say “I do"? After only one pair of contestants tied the knot in season eight, Love Is Blind executive producer Ally Simpson provided insight into why it’s getting more rare for couples to make it past the altar, admitting that social media has made it challenging to recruit “nice men” for the experiment. She also noted that former contestant Chelsea Griffin joined the casting team to help mitigate the issue.

22. There were rumors online that Mark Cuevas, the youngest single man in the inaugural season, secretly had a girlfriend when he was on the show, which he later denied.

"I did not have a girlfriend the whole time," he said on the Be There in Five podcast. "I was single before [going on the show]. Now, being a single man, you're single, you're doing your thing. I'd never had a girlfriend before. Where was the time to be in a relationship during the experience?"

23. According to Kelly and Kenny Barnes, they never had any plans to actually get married at the end of filming season one. "We were adamant about we're not going to get married," Kenny told People. "And really, the engagement was just to extend the experiment. And we were both committed to that." And Kelly believed they were "going to continue dating, because that was communicated between us." That was until Kenny admitted on the wedding day he was not "emotionally available" to do so. "I felt very rejected," she admitted. "Like, 'That wasn't the plan. What the heck?'"

24. Jessica Batten, LIB season one's most polarizing cast member, claimed in an interview that she tried to quit the show but wasn't allowed to.

"I mean, I had to stay," she told EW. "I definitely had a conversation about leaving and I wasn't able to do that. My dog got sick too, and almost died during the show, I had so much other stuff going on."

She continued, "It was really frustrating because I kind of knew [Mark and I] weren't going to get there. I definitely had some conversations and attempted to leave, but I wasn't able to."

25. The season one weddings were actually filmed in the fall of 2018, meaning the two couples—Lauren and Cameron Hamilton, and Amber Pike and Matt Barnett—who actually decided to get married had to keep their relationships secret for 18 months before the show premiered.

26. Love Is Blind could go on and on for years, with Coelen telling E! News, "I'm thinking season 15" would be the last.

27. Did Carlton Morton look familiar? Well, it's because he's been on an ATL-set reality show before, popping up on The Real Housewives of Atlanta when he was working as Cynthia Bailey's assistant back in 2014. Bravo fans will remember he got into a heated argument with Kenya Moore, who had just joined the series.

28. Why does the show always have those gold cups? While fans have speculated the opaque cups help hide the amount of liquid in the glass to help with editing errors and cuts, Coelen said the goblets were chosen for aesthetics.

"When you turn on the show, you know it's our show," he told Variety. "It's a very authentic, really true following of these people's journeys, but I like the fact that we have this sort of connective tissue with that in a really light way, it's fun."

Deepti added to E! News, "We would always put all of our drinks in the gold goblets. It's just a symbolic thing for us."

XPENG Sales Soar!

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

XPENG had a fantabulous sales month in October. We can give you the numbers, but as you can see well enough in the chart above and below, the company’s sales soared to a whole new level last month.

Reaching 50,237 vehicle deliveries, that meant 22% sales growth month over month and 20% sales growth year over year. There were 41,256 XPENG vehicles delivered in September 2026 and 42,013 delivered in October 2025, the company’s previous monthly record. Will it be another year until XPENG beats its monthly sales record again, or is the October 2026 result just the beginning of more record sales in coming months?

Well, there are some reasons to expect even more growth. “In October 2026, the XPENG L05 was officially launched in the Chinese mainland, with deliveries set to begin on November 1, 2026,” XPENG shares. “On October 26, 2026, XPENG obtained a demonstration operation license to conduct Level 4 Robotaxi services in Guangzhou. The approval enables XPENG Robotaxi to advance from technology validation to real-world operational and business model validation, marking a key milestone toward the commercialization of XPENG’s Physical AI technologies.”

A big portion of this sales growth came from tremendous growth in sales outside of XPENG’s home market of China. “Overseas deliveries also reached an all-time monthly high of 13,326 units, up 166% year-over-year,” the company shares.

Overall, things are looking very bright for XPENG. And those EV sales are helping the planet and humanity as well. “XPENG’s electric vehicles delivered from January to October 2026 are expected to reduce life-cycle greenhouse gas emissions by more than 5.77 million tons compared to internal combustion engine vehicles, equivalent to the carbon absorbed by 95.43 million young trees over 10 years,” XPENG wrote in closing, and I’ll close with that highlight as well.

Oh, wait, I’ll also add that XPENG’s big spike in sales in October helped the company to shoot high above similar (but different) rival NIO in their long-term battle as the best selling smart EV startup.

NIO didn’t have its best month, but maybe it will surge again in November and/or December.
